Accepting it: BLDesk stayed open until the password was accepted, closed about a second later and reopened by itself on the new version. The AppArmor profile was not removed (the kernel logged only `profile_replace`, "same as current profile, skipping"); the one upgrade that ran the old beta.31 script still showed `profile_remove` and `profile_load` 6.2 s apart. The post-install script took 0.06 s after the MIME change (6.0 s before it), and BLDesk was closed for 3.1 s from the window going to the new one running (9.3 s with the MIME step, and about 11.5 s of frozen window, then two prompts, with the original flow). The re-check before installing took 12 ms.
- Other distributions, in KVM virtual machines on the same machine (QEMU 10.2 run from unpacked packages, cloud images, headless unless stated): Debian 12 (polkit 122, apt 2.6.1), Debian 13 (polkit 126, apt 3.0.3), Ubuntu 22.04.5 (polkit 0.105, apt 2.4.14) and Ubuntu 24.04.5 (polkit 124, apt 2.8.3). In each, twice: the real package from this branch installs with its dependencies (155 to 198 packages); polkit knows the action and reports "Authentication is required to install a BLDesk update" with `auth_admin` for an active session; the helper refuses a wrong checksum, a symbolic link, a file owned by someone else, a package that is not `bldesk` and a checksum that is not base64, leaving nothing in `/var/tmp`; the real `pkexec` runs the real helper, which upgrades 1.0.62~beta.31.3 to .4 and prints AUTHORIZED, then INSTALLED; and asking it to go back to .3 ends in apt's own "Packages were downgraded and -y was used without --allow-downgrades". The headless VMs cannot complete polkit's password step (plain `pkexec id` fails there too, with "No session for cookie"), so in them a test-only rule authorises this one action for the test user; the password step itself was exercised on the real desktop and in the KDE machine below. On the two guests whose kernel runs AppArmor (Debian 13 and Ubuntu 24.04) the profile was still loaded after the upgrade, with no `profile_remove` event.
- KDE Plasma 5.27 on X11 (Ubuntu 24.04 with `kde-plasma-desktop`, automatic login, software rendering), driven by screenshots and key presses through QEMU's monitor: the real `update-session.sh` ran inside the Plasma session, and KDE's polkit agent showed a dialog titled "Authentication is required to install a BLDesk update". Escape made `pkexec` exit 127 ("Not authorized"; GNOME's agent gives 126), and the wrapper wrote `RESULT cancelled`. Typing the password wrote AUTHORIZED, INSTALLED and `RESULT installed`, and the package went from beta.31.3 to beta.31.4. A plain Plasma install had no `pkexec` (Ubuntu ships it as its own package), and the wrapper then reported a cancelled prompt; it now writes `RESULT unavailable pkexec (part of polkit)` before any prompt, and the same for a missing `apt-get` or `dpkg-deb` (3 new tests, 7 mutations caught).
- Not tested: the quit-time install with the real prompt; a download from GitHub's feed rather than the mock; Wayland sessions on desktops other than GNOME, and the XFCE, Cinnamon, MATE and LXQt agents; Debian 11 and older; a non-Debian system running a repackaged `.deb`; and no polkit agent running at all (`pkexec` exits 127 and it is reported as a closed prompt).

## Self-audit and independent review of the Linux update flow (21 October 2026)

After the work above was pushed as a draft, the whole diff was audited, in a throwaway virtual machine where it needed a real Linux system: a read of every changed file, adversarial tests of the root helper (a race and a size bomb, run as root against a local attacker process), a clean checkout built and tested from scratch, and a separate reviewer that was given only the diff and no conclusions. The reviewer found the same two helper problems independently and several more. What was found, and what was done:

| Finding | Evidence | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **The helper could be made to copy a root-only file and leave the copy readable.** It checked the package (regular file, not a link, owner, size), then read it as root and made the copy world-readable before comparing its checksum. A local process swapping the path for a link to a root-only file in between got that file copied and readable for a few milliseconds. | Against the pushed helper: 17,044 runs in 90 s, the root-only file was copied 4,359 times (its own checksum matched) and an unprivileged reader read it 80,161 times. | The package is now read **as the requesting user** through `setpriv` (a swapped path then reads nothing the user could not), into a folder only root can enter, and the copy is opened to `apt-get` only after the checksum and package-name checks pass. After: 5,181 runs on the same test, no copy of the file, no read; the same 20-second test on Debian 12 and 13 and Ubuntu 22.04 and 24.04 (290 to 2,115 runs each): none. |
| The size limit was a `stat` before the copy. | A 1,500 MiB sparse file swapped in after the check was copied in full into `/var/tmp` (the limit is 500 MiB). | At most 500 MiB plus one byte is read, and the copy is measured. 500 MiB peak on all five systems. |
| The scratch folder was left behind when the helper was killed during its wait. | Found by the reviewer (the `EXIT` trap does not run on `SIGTERM`). | `HUP`, `INT` and `TERM` now run it; a test kills the helper while it waits. |
| The wrapper handed `pkexec` whatever `install-update.sh` sat beside it, including one in a user's own folder (an unpacked or development copy). | Reviewer, by reading. | The wrapper refuses (`RESULT untrusted`) unless the helper and every folder above it belong to root and are not writable by group or others. `after-install.sh` makes `/opt/BLDesk`, `resources` and `resources/linux` 755 whatever umask built the package (a package built here comes out 775, and installing it changed them to 755). |
| If the session script died without a result (killed, logged out), the status stayed on "installing" for good: no Restart, no check, no quit-time install. | Reviewer. Reproduced: with `pkexec` replaced by a stand-in that hangs and the session script killed, the real app returned to `ready` with "the installer stopped unexpectedly (SIGKILL)". | BLDesk now writes `RESULT failed` itself when the session script exits without a verdict. |
| BLDesk was reopened after a dismissed prompt if the user had quit it meanwhile. | Reviewer; a test asserted that behaviour. | The wrapper reopens BLDesk only after `AUTHORIZED` (what tells it to close), and waits for it to finish closing. |
| An install that finished while BLDesk was still open left the package offered again, so quitting started a second session, and the comment said the installer reopens BLDesk, which it did not. | Reviewer. | The package is dropped before the quit, and the wrapper's wait makes the reopen true. |
| A newer release announced by a check that outlived the six-second re-check could replace the state, and the download, under an open prompt. | Reviewer, by reading. | While a session runs such announcements are ignored and automatic download is off. |
| A file the helper refused (wrong checksum and so on) stayed offered, and was refused again. | Reviewer. Reproduced with a few bytes of the cached file changed: refused for the right reason, nothing installed, the file deleted and downloaded again, `ready` again. | Dropped and downloaded again. |
| A newer version that finished downloading while the re-check was still running left the flag that installs on arrival set, so a later check could install without being asked. | Self-audit, by reading. | Installs at once in that case, and a fresh Restart clears the flag. |
| **Release blocker:** `test-linux-package-scripts` runs `bash` with Linux paths and symlinks, and `release.yml` runs the whole typecheck on `windows-2022` and `macos-latest` as well as Ubuntu. Pull request CI only runs on Ubuntu, so it passed. | Reviewer (a guess, confirmed from `release.yml`). | Those tests skip off Linux, as the new script tests already did; `updateScriptPaths` uses `posix.join` so its test is the same on Windows. |
| Several checks could be deleted from the helper with every test still passing: the size check, the newline check, the base64 check, `DPkg::Lock::Timeout`, the `PATH` reset. The test named for the folder permissions only compared two strings in the source. | Reviewer, by mutation. | A test for each, and each refusal test now asserts its own reason; the folder permissions are observed while the copy is checked. 19 deliberate breakages of the fixed scripts, tests and guards: all caught (one guard needed tightening first). |
| `Recommends` for the tool the flow needs; an unmatched glob would abort the post-install script; documentation overstated the old copy step and counted 34 tests. | Reviewer. | `Recommends: pkexec | policykit-1`, a loop in place of the glob, and the text corrected. |

**Found in review after the audit (22 October 2026).** The maintainer's review of the pull request pointed out that the root-opened copy was handed to the unprivileged `head` as its output, so a process of the same user that may attach to `head` could keep that handle and write to the copy after the checksum check, which breaks "swapping the file after the check changes nothing". Reproduced in a VM before fixing: with Debian's default ptrace setting (`kernel.yama.ptrace_scope=0`) an unprivileged process took `head`'s output with `pidfd_getfd`, waited for the folder to be opened to `apt-get`, and wrote a different package into the copy; the helper installed that package in 6 of 6 runs, although only the other package's checksum had been checked. With Ubuntu's default (1) a process that is not an ancestor of `head` was refused (4 of 4); an ancestor, which BLDesk is, is allowed. Fixed by piping `head` into root's own `cat > "$COPY"` (plus a `test -r` as the user first, since `sh` has no `pipefail`): the attacker still takes the handle, but it is a pipe, the write fails (`EINVAL`), and the package that was checked is installed (5 of 5 runs here, 6 of 6 on the first variant). A test now asserts the unprivileged process's output is a pipe, and a guard fails if the output of a `setpriv` command is redirected into a file. The race and size-bomb tests were run again against the fixed helper: nothing leaked in 772 runs, and the copy stopped at 500 MiB.

Real runs after the fixes, in a VM on Ubuntu 24.04 with the real Electron app under Xvfb, the real `pkexec`, helper and `apt-get` (the only substitute is a test-only polkit rule for the password step): accepted, from 31.3 to 31.4, with the app closing, the real install and BLDesk reopening on its own, no scratch left; no rule and no agent, where `pkexec` exits 127 and BLDesk stayed open with the "closed or not accepted" notice; the tampered download; and the killed session script. The KDE Plasma run was repeated with the same packages: a plain Plasma install had no `pkexec` until the BLDesk package was installed, which brought it in through `Recommends`; the wrapper's trust check passed on the installed layout (everything from `/opt` down root-owned, 755); KDE's dialog appeared, Escape gave `RESULT cancelled`, and typing the password gave `RESULT installed` and the move from 31.3 to 31.4. The four-distribution run (Debian 12 and 13, Ubuntu 22.04 and 24.04) was repeated with the packages built in that VM from a clean checkout: installs with dependencies, real `pkexec` upgrade, downgrade refused, the race and the size bomb as above, and the AppArmor profile still loaded after the upgrade on Debian 13 and Ubuntu 24.04. The helper also ran a real install under `bash` as `/bin/sh` and under `dash`, and `shellcheck` is clean on the helper and the session script.

After the review's fixes (22 October 2026), the four-distribution run was repeated with the packages built from the merged branch; the helper and the session script inside them were compared with the branch's, byte for byte (SHA-256). Installs with dependencies, the helper's five refusals, the real `pkexec` upgrade, the downgrade refused, and the AppArmor profile still loaded after the upgrade on Debian 13 and Ubuntu 24.04 all came out as before. The race left nothing readable in 259 runs (Debian 12), 2027 (Debian 13), 330 (Ubuntu 22.04) and 1923 (Ubuntu 24.04), and the size bomb never made a copy larger than 500 MiB (119 to 168 runs each). The review's open-handle attack was run on each distribution with its own default ptrace setting, 4 runs each, against the helper as it was before the fix and against the one in the package. On Debian 12 and 13 (`ptrace_scope` 0) the old helper installed the package the attacker wrote (31.4) in 4 of 4 runs, although only 31.3's checksum had been checked; the packaged helper let the attacker take the handle in every run, the write failed with `EINVAL`, and 31.3 was installed in 4 of 4. On Ubuntu 22.04 and 24.04 (`ptrace_scope` 1) `pidfd_getfd` was refused to a process that is not an ancestor, and both helpers installed 31.3 in 4 of 4. The KDE run was not repeated: it exercised KDE's polkit dialog and `update-session.sh`, and neither changed (the helper's change went through the real `pkexec` on all four distributions above).

**Not fixed, and why.**
- A process running as the user can ask for the same prompt with a package of its own. The checksum is supplied by the caller, so it only guards against swaps. Closing this needs a signature the helper verifies against a key shipped root-owned in the package (or an APT repository), which needs release signing that is not set up here. It is now stated plainly in `docs/AUTO_UPDATE.md`.
- A quit-time session still waiting for its password when BLDesk is opened again is left alone, so a Restart can open a second prompt (a session pid file would fix it). Another package manager holding the dpkg lock keeps BLDesk closed for up to two minutes. A shutdown during `apt-get` leaves dpkg half-way, as for any interrupted install. All three are in the "Known limits" paragraph of `docs/AUTO_UPDATE.md`.
- Nits left as they are: the helper's error text differs by path state, `installError` is only cleared on the next install or check.

**The state machine's tests (22 October 2026).** The maintainer's review asked for the install flow's decisions to be tested without Electron. They are now `src/main/linuxInstallFlow.ts`, a pure function from the state and one event to the new state and effects, and `updater.ts` only gathers the facts and carries the effects out; the Windows, macOS and AppImage paths in `updater.ts` are back to exactly what `main` had (the guard on `markReady` and the changes to `handleCheckError` that I had made for all platforms now apply to Linux packages only). `scripts/test-linux-install-flow.mjs` has 42 tests with a small simulated world (timers fire when time is advanced, no waiting): Restart during a check, a newer release found during the look at the feed (still downloading, and already downloaded), cancel then Restart again, a release announced while the prompt is open (including after a slow feed's timeout), the session script dying without a verdict, and the install at quit, with the guards behind each. I removed each of 36 guards in turn; 34 of the 36 tests failed at once, and the other two showed two real gaps (a second Restart while a newer release had made the update look ready again, and a stray close timer), which now have tests; all 36 are then caught. `check-updater-guards.mjs` keeps the structure: Restart only ever produces the look, a session for Restart only follows the look's end, and `updater.ts` starts the session script only as the flow's effect. Real runs after the refactor, in the VM with the real app, `pkexec`, helper and `apt-get`: accepted; no polkit agent; a tampered download; the session script killed; and the feed offering a newer release after the download, where Restart went from "checking" straight to the prompt for the newer version and installed it, with BLDesk reopening on its own.
